| Code width bounded by failure domains; no code below 1.5x survives a node on three nodes | LRC (ATC 2012: local groups need many domains); Ceph EC profiles (failure domain as a profile property); arithmetic of 10.4 | none needed |
| Per-shard checksums, scrub, recompute-and-rewrite repair, prioritized re-replication | GFS (SOSP 2003), Backblaze Vaults, Ceph scrub | C-11 scrub rate and foreground cost |
| Redundancy chosen per tier by configuration, declustered repair with spread spare space | Colossus (2021 post: client-chosen encodings, custodians), f4 rebuilders, Backblaze | C-11 rebuild rate at `rebuild.share` |
| Ranged GETs of 8 to 16 MiB and many concurrent requests to saturate a storage service | Durner, Leis, Neumann (VLDB 2023), AWS S3 guidelines | C-1, C-4 (part size P and first-part size on our drives) |
| One or two sequential streams per spindle, long slices, prefetch depth matched to seek cost | Li, Shen, Papathanasiou (EuroSys 2007, competitive prefetching); Iyer and Druschel (SOSP 2001, anticipatory scheduling); Schindler et al. (FAST 2002); Ruemmler and Wilkes (1994) | C-2, C-2b, C-8 (streams, chunk, slice, write share, slot release) |
| HDD efficiency by request size on our drives | T0 [M]: 119 MB/s sequential, about 43 MB/s (36%) at 1 MiB scattered on an ST8000DM004 | T0-S on the SAS drives |
| Cluster-comparable order of records and tombstones | Hybrid logical clocks (Kulkarni et al., OPODIS 2014) | the simulation tests of section 14 |
| One agreed sealed length under partitions | Paxos (Lamport, 2001), single decree | the partition simulation of section 14 |
| sha256 cost on build nodes without SHA-NI | T0 [M]: about 290 MB/s per core with `ring`, 150 MB/s with `sha2` | T-FMT (blake3 cells, if ever) |
| Loop-device cache | T0 [M]: `O_DIRECT` absorbed by the page cache, buffered writers stalled more than 20 s | T0 B on the partition |
| Hot-tier group-commit latency, PLP, preallocation | not published for our devices | T0-S NVMe items (a stop condition without PLP) |
| Promotion thresholds, expiry-class grouping, GC rewrite threshold | no published data for this workload | C-3b, C-10 |
| Embedded index store at 1e8 keys | vendor benchmarks only | C-9, T-S3 |
| SMR behaviour under sealed-shard churn | drive-managed SMR behaviour is vendor-specific | C-5b (runs in full on NOW) |
| FUSE transport (`/dev/fuse` clones, io_uring) | kernel documentation; our baselines (2,769 and 28,199 opens/s) | 17.1 |
| Prefetch order for a lazily fetched tree | eStargz (prioritized files and a landmark, recorded by an optimize run at image build time) and Nydus (a prefetch table in the bootstrap, from build-time hints): both decide the order at build time, not from runs | 17.3 decides whether a publish-time hint (6.1) is worth building |
| Hedged requests at about the p95 cut tail latency for about 5% extra load | Dean and Barroso, "The Tail at Scale", CACM 2013 | C-3 (hedge to the other replica against a loaded drive), T10b |
| 1 MiB cells | arithmetic of section 2 (digest size, hash rate [M], drive rate [I], FUSE read size [run, K21]) | T-F0 (`max_pages`, done), T10b first byte |

## 23. The client as libraries, the daemon as a thin host [D]
**Why.** The read path of sections 2 to 9 is useful outside the daemon: a tool that reads one file of a tree, a benchmark that measures the storage tier without FUSE, a scheduler that prefetches in its own process, later a Python or TypeScript program. So the client is three layered crates, each usable alone, and the daemon is one program built on them with nothing of its own on the read path.

| Crate | What it is | What it is not | Depends on |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`tierstore-client`** | Membership (seeds or file, epoch following; 10.8); batched `LOCATE` with single flight per key (10.6); ranged part fetch: the part grid, ramp and first piece (4.4), the queue with classes, DRR and ranks (4.5), stall detection, retries, hedges, the pinned generation and `If-Match` (4.6); per-cell verification with the hash pool (3.3, 4.7); the budget (4.3); the definitive-miss rule and the lost outcome (4.6, 10.6). Its output is verified cells delivered into the caller's buffers. | no cache, no files on disk, no state page, no daemon, no FUSE; it does not dedupe two callers' fetches of one pack (that is the cache's single flight). | `tierstore-fetch-api`, `tierstore-store-api` (protocol types), `tierstore-format` |
| **`tierstore-cache`** | The local cache: the entry store (sparse files, cell states, the sealed state memfd on Linux and the in-process store on macOS; 2, 5.1, 5.6), single flight and claims (`PackFill`, `ensure`, `wait`; 4.1, 4.2), holds, eviction and admission (7), the journal and restart (8). In-process by default; **shareable across processes** on Linux through the existing mechanism of 5.1 and 5.4: one owning process holds the `flock`s and serves `open-pack`, `wait`, `ensure` and `release` on a unix socket (the server side, T5), and any other process attaches as a holder (the client side, T6), maps the state page read-only and reads pack bytes from the passed data fd with the reader protocol of 5.1. | no FUSE, no tree format, no network code of its own: claimed cells are fetched by `tierstore-client` | `tierstore-client`, `tierstore-fetch-api` |
| **`tierstore-tree`** | Open an L2 tree list (3; from the cache, or from bytes the client fetched) and read by path: `lookup`, `read_dir`, `stat`, `read_link`, `read(file, offset, buf)`, from the cache (an in-process or attached `tierstore-cache`) or **straight from the client** (verified cells into the caller's buffer, no entry on disk); the mapped-image serving of 5.3 (binary search of sorted directory records, WILLNEED, optional `mlock`); the planner of section 6 (T7, a pure function). | no FUSE and no NFS: the fronts are adapters on it | `tierstore-client`; `tierstore-cache` behind the default cargo feature `cache`, so a tool can link the tree reader with the client alone |

**The daemon (`tierstore`) is a host.** It reads the configuration (10.14), starts the enabled components of 10.1 and wires them: the FUSE front (`TreeFs`, an adapter of `tierstore-tree` onto `tsfuse::Fs`: opcodes, parked replies, NO_OPEN, splice; each mount runs in a mount process of the same binary that attaches to the node-wide shared cache through `cache.shared_socket`, as 5.1 and 5.4 describe), the NFS front (the same adapter onto the NFS handlers), a node-wide shared `tierstore-cache` with its socket, the S3 front (section 9's routing of large objects into the cache's engine), the Prepare provider (the jobscheduler provider protocol onto `tierstore-tree`'s planner and the cache's `ensure`), and, when enabled, the store components of section 10, unchanged by this section. **Every read the fronts and Prepare make goes through the public API of the three crates**; the daemon has no private fetch path, no second cache and no second tree reader. A layering check in `cargo xtask` (T-L) fails when a front or the Prepare provider of the daemon imports `tierstore-store-api`'s peer protocol, a non-public module of the three crates, or the entry store directly, and its negative case is a fixture module that does each of the three and must be refused.

**Who uses the crates.**
- **The daemon:** all three, as above.
- **jobscheduler, Prepare in-process:** a jobscheduler agent on a build node links `tierstore-tree` and attaches to the node-wide shared cache (`tierstore-cache`, client side), and runs the planner and `ensure` in its own process at job assignment, without the provider round trip; the daemon's Prepare provider is the same library code behind the provider socket, for agents that do not link it. Both take the same holds (6, item 7).
- **The build system (the farm's build executor):** reading an action's input or output tree without a mount, through `tierstore-tree` with the client alone. Its own rules admit no dependency on code of ours today, so it adopts the crates only after those rules name them as allowed; until then it reads through the mount, and nothing in this design depends on it linking them.
- **Tools:** `tierstore verify` (re-derives per-file sha256 from cells, 3.4), `tierstore store audit` (10.8), and a `tierstore tree cat <tree> <path>` verb that reads one file through the client with no daemon.
- **Benchmarks:** T-B5 and T0-S read through `tierstore-client` directly, so the storage tier is measured without FUSE or the cache in the way; the FUSE benchmarks (17.1) keep their harness.
- **Later, Python and TypeScript bindings** over `tierstore-tree` and `tierstore-client` (not designed here; the stable API below is what makes them possible).

**A stable, documented, versioned API.** Each crate has its own version (not the workspace's): 0.x from T-L, while the tasks of section 18 fill it, and 1.0.0 when T10b has frozen the constants and T-F8 serves through it; from 1.0.0, within a major version its public API changes additively only (a new item, a new optional field behind a constructor, never a removed, renamed or retyped item), and a breaking change is a new major version with a note in this document. Every public item is documented (`#![deny(missing_docs)]`), each crate's README shows the use alone (client only; cache with a client; tree straight from the client), and errors are `thiserror` enums with context (`ClientError`, `CacheError`, `TreeError`; `MembershipUnavailable`, `ListVersionUnsupported`, `Lost` and `NotFound` are named variants, never strings). Each crate carries an **API test** (`tests/api_v1.rs`) that names every public item of its major version with its full signature, so a removal, rename or retype fails to compile; a PR that adds an item adds it to the test. The traits of `tierstore-fetch-api` (`Entry`, `EntryStore`, `Engine`, `Budget`, `Verifier`, `Upstream`, `ListL2`) stay the interfaces the crates implement and the fakes stand in for; the crates' own APIs are the concrete types callers use. The interface crates of T-I (`tierstore-fetch-api`, `tierstore-store-api`, `tierstore-format`) are not part of this promise: they change only through a freeze delta of section 18 (such as T-I2), with its wire and format changes additive (B8 of AGENTS.md).

**Library-mode test suite (T-L).** Behaviour tests that run the crates with no daemon, against the fake storage node of `tierstore-store-api` on a loopback listener at port 0: the client alone fetches and verifies a pack and reads a `digest=none` object; the client bootstraps from seeds (one seed down, one answering `404`, a wrong token) and follows a higher epoch; the cache in-process fills, holds, evicts and restarts with the client; a second process attaches to a shared cache, reads through the state page and survives the owner's restart (the epoch and reconnect rules of 5.4); the tree reads every file of a test tree by path from the cache and straight from the client, with byte-for-byte equal results; the planner ranks a test tree shared packs first, then in tree order. These are rows of section 14.

**Mapping of the client-side tasks onto the crates.** The tasks keep their scope and tests (section 18); this is where their code lives.

| Task | Crate and module |
|---|---|
| T1 entry store (Linux) | `tierstore-cache` (`entry::linux`) |
| T-M1 in-process entry store; NFS handlers | `tierstore-cache` (`entry::inproc`); the NFS adapter in the daemon on `tierstore-tree` |
| T1b journal and snapshot | `tierstore-cache` (`journal`) |
| T2 hash pool | `tierstore-client` (`verify`) |
| T3 fetch engine | split: the part queue, grid and ramp, demand sub-ranges, stall retries, hedges, pinned generation and `If-Match`, the `LOCATE` step and the lost outcome in `tierstore-client` (`fetch`); single flight, claims and the `Engine` implementation (`ensure`, `wait`) in `tierstore-cache` (`fill`) |
| T4 budget | `tierstore-client` (`budget`) |
| T5 server side of the mount protocol | `tierstore-cache` (`share::server`); the daemon only binds the socket |
| T6 mount client | `tierstore-cache` (`share::client`), used by mount processes and by any process attaching to the shared cache |
| T7 Prepare planner | `tierstore-tree` (`plan`) |
| T8 eviction and admission | `tierstore-cache` (`evict`, `admit`) |
| T9 metrics | each crate registers its metrics on a registry handle its constructor takes (no global registry); the daemon exports them |
| T-F8 tree `Fs` on `tsfuse` | split: path lookup, image serving and the read path with the post-read recheck in `tierstore-tree` (`Tree`); the `tsfuse::Fs` adapter (`TreeFs`) in the daemon |
| T12 S3-front integration | the daemon, on `tierstore-cache` |
| T-S6 `StoreUpstream` (the client side of the peer protocol) | `tierstore-client` (`store`); the peer service stays with the store components |
| T-SB seed bootstrap | `tierstore-client` (`membership`); the route in the peer service (T-S6) |

## 24. Operations and repair [D]
This section is the operations and repair design: who decides that hardware is gone, what is repaired in which order, how a returning node or drive is reconciled, how capacity is added, what the operator runs, and the runbooks. It was written after a study of Ceph, Swift, Dynamo and Cassandra, HDFS, GFS, f4, Backblaze, MinIO and SeaweedFS and an adversarial failure review; every blocker the review named is resolved in the text below and listed in 24.14. Labels as elsewhere: [D] decided here, [E] arithmetic, [I] inference that a named test or measurement settles. Constants are [I] until T0-S and the simulator (24.13) tune them.

**Decisions.**
- **D1. The block is the placement group.** The unit of placement, repair, audit and rebalance is the recorded block (a hot segment or large object, a cold stripe). No placement groups are layered over keys, and `rank(K)` is a routing hint and tie-break, never a correctness input (24.1).
- **D2. A health ledger of single-decree facts.** Obligation-changing facts (lost, returned, maintenance, flapping) are majority-committed one fact at a time, with the same single-decree agreement the seal and descriptor commits use, so no log, leader or consensus service is needed (24.2).
- **D3. Two clocks and a damper.** Short detection only reroutes reads; a long, explicit, ledger-recorded `lost` decision moves a node's repair from "margin-driven" to "rebuild everything"; flap damping is a preference with a floor (24.3).
- **D4. A soft-state repair scheduler** recomputed from descriptors, the block table, the ledger and drive states, ordered by remaining margin, with idempotent commit steps and **one throttle budget shared with scrub** (24.5).
- **D5. Degraded placement is a recorded exception with a debt counter** (`layout_debt`), reverted by a mover or by adopting the returning node's copy (24.4, 24.5).
- **D6. Adding capacity never moves existing data** unless an operator applies a rebalance (24.8).
- **D7. Ten audited invariants**, each with a metric, a check and an alert (24.10).
- **D8. Operator verbs are generated from the operator API**, destructive ones dry-run by default and refuse unsafe states (24.11).
- **D9. A deterministic cluster simulator is the primary test**; farm drills confirm what it cannot (24.13).

**The three-node fact (stated once, applies everywhere).** With three storage nodes every majority is two. While one node is down, **every seal, every descriptor commit (every repair commit) and every ledger fact needs both survivors**; one slow or `stalled` survivor stops all commits until it recovers, and the metric `repair_commit_quorum_stalled` (seconds with a committable item waiting for a quorum) pages after 10 minutes. After a node is marked `out`, the two remaining nodes read every `4+4` stripe at margin 0 and **cannot commit anything if one more node dies**. Rebuilding onto two nodes is an interim state: **a fourth storage node is the fix** for sustained operation after a node loss.

### 24.1 Placement: the block, not the key
- **Where key K lives:** in the index, `K -> (block id, offset, length, generation)` (10.2), on every node that indexes K.
- **Where block B lives:** hot, the replica set recorded in the block table when the segment opened (changed only by a committed re-replication record); cold, the stripe descriptor's shard list. Reads always use the recorded location.
- **What rendezvous still does:** choose among eligible live candidates at decision time, never locate existing data: (1) which node a client asks first for `LOCATE` (10.6); (2) the targets of new segments, new shards and repair writes (weighted by free bytes over `in`, not-`flapping`, below-fill-limit candidates, 10.4); (3) the coordinator of a repair item or the proposer of a ledger fact: `rank(item id)` among live nodes, the next rank taking over after a timeout, which gives every item a deterministic owner without an election.
- **Why no placement groups.** A placement group exists to bound the number of independent decisions; the 1 GiB block already does: at the capacity guard LATER's cold tier is about 133,000 shard files, about 16,600 `4+4` stripes, about 2,950 shards per drive [E], the same order as the per-device group counts Ceph recommends, and the hot tier has at most tens to hundreds of segments.
- **The versioned map.** Two documents with epochs: the **membership document** (operator intent, epoch `M`, 10.8) and the **health ledger** (runtime facts, 24.2, epoch `L`). Peer messages carry both (`x-ts-mem-epoch` exists; `x-ts-ledger-epoch` is added, T-I2). A receiver that sees a higher epoch pulls the difference before it acts. **Epoch refusals are by content, not by number:** a placement (a new segment, shard or repair target) planned under an older map is refused (`409 epoch-behind` with the diff) only when the difference changes the eligibility of that item's target or sources; an unrelated fact (a `flapping` fact about another node, another drive's `lost`) never restarts in-flight work. Reads are never refused for epoch skew.
- **Recorded exceptions** are fields of the block record: `layout_debt` (the stripe sits in a degraded layout, 24.5) and `move_to` (a pending move). The recorded location always serves reads.
- **Repair groups, for digests only.** Blocks are grouped `g = blake3(block_id) mod 256`, fixed forever. Each node keeps per group a digest of `(block id, state, copy locations, descriptor epoch)`; peers compare the 256 digests every 10 minutes and drill into a mismatching group. This detects block-table divergence cheaply (invariant I10); bytes are the scrubber's (24.7).

### 24.2 States and the health ledger
**Granularity.** State is per component: a node's daemon, its hot device, and each of its drives. A drive's identity is a UUID written into its `DRIVE` file at format time and mounted by UUID, so renames and slot moves are recognised. A node's identity is `node.id` plus an **incarnation**: a random 64-bit value written into the `NODE` file, kept across restarts and regenerated when the node starts on a fresh hot device (T-I2 adds it to the identity file).

**States added to 10.8.**

| State | Meaning | Decided by | Reads | New placements | Repair effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `new` | in the membership document, no data, not yet verified | owner (format check, then an 8 GiB write-and-verify burn) | no | no | none; becomes `up` |
| `missing` (drive) | absent at boot or gone from the bus | owner | not asked | no | lost after `rebuild.drive_missing_after` (1 h); a returning drive clears it |
| `flapping` | the damper tripped (24.3) | each viewer, deterministically from recorded transitions; a ledger fact after 1 h | last in preference | not preferred, with a floor (24.3) | its returned-processing is held |
| `maintenance` | the operator said "away on purpose until T" | ledger fact | as `down` | no | the lost budget does not run inside the window; at T the node is `down` from T |
| `lost` | repair obligations for every block on this component are active | ledger fact | not asked | no | every block listing it is queued for full rebuild |

`indexing` (10.8) keeps its meaning for client traffic only: **acceptor duty is served in `indexing`** (promise, accept and state fetch for seals, descriptors and ledger facts, from persisted state), independent of the index rebuild, so a node that restarts can vote at once and a power event does not wait for index rebuilds before any seal can commit (except under the amnesia rule below).

**The health ledger.** A **fact** is `(subject, generation, kind, params)`; a subject is a node incarnation or a drive UUID; kinds are `lost`, `returned`, `maintenance(until)`, `maintenance-end`, `flapping`, `flapping-end`, `failed-cleared`. The generation is the number of facts already committed for the subject, and a subject's state is its fact with the highest committed generation. Each fact is decided by **one single-decree majority agreement** over the ledger voters, instance id `(subject, generation)`. Facts about different subjects commute; the ledger epoch `L` is the count of committed facts. The ledger is small (tens of facts a year) and is pulled whole by any node that is behind.

- **Proposers.** The node that wins `rank(subject)` among live voters proposes; the next rank proposes after `ledger.takeover` (60 s). An acceptor that currently sees the subject `up`, and has not itself seen it unavailable for the timer, **rejects** a `lost` proposal, so a `lost` fact needs a majority that independently agrees. A partitioned minority cannot commit anything, cannot acknowledge `min_acks = 2` hot writes, and fences itself; when it heals it learns any fact about itself and rejoins by 24.4.
- **Voter sets [D].** The **ledger voters** are an explicit list in the membership document (`ledger = true` on each voter node; all three storage nodes on LATER; three nodes on NOW). The **seal majority** is over the segment's recorded replica set (10.3), and the **descriptor majority** over the nodes of the stripe's recorded placement, not over "all nodes in the document". Adding a node therefore changes no existing majority. The ledger voter set changes by **one node at a time**, and only after the new voter has synced the ledger; an acceptor refuses a ledger message whose sender's membership epoch disagrees with its own on the voter set.
- **No amnesiac voting [D].** A node whose hot device is new (a fresh incarnation), or whose acceptor state comes from anything but its own continuous persisted state (a restored image, a snapshot), is **non-voting for every instance it has any record of**, and for every ledger instance up to the current epoch, until it has learned the committed state of those instances from a majority that is reachable now. If no such majority is reachable (for example node A down and node B's NVMe replaced: the only majority would be {B', C}), the cluster **stops committing and waits**: a safe stall, never a second decision. Snapshots are used only for the data catalogue (block table, index), never for acceptor or ledger state.
- **If the ledger cannot commit** (two of three nodes away), nothing is declared; the fallback is the operator's membership edit (24.9). The ledger is an optimisation of decisions, never a safety dependency of data: descriptor commits and seals have their own majority rules.

### 24.3 Timers, damping and guards
**Detection (per viewer; reroutes only).** As 10.8: storage nodes judge peers from their own traffic and `x-ts-load`, and ping only an idle peer (10.6); `down` after 3 silent intervals (6 s); a client judges only from its own requests. `stalled` past `stall_lost_after` (30 min) becomes `failed`.

**Decision (ledger).**

| Timer | Default | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `rebuild.drive_delay` | 10 min | a `failed` drive becomes `lost` |
| `rebuild.drive_missing_after` | 1 h | a `missing` drive becomes `lost` |
| `rebuild.node_down_after` | 24 h within a trailing 48 h, cumulative | a node becomes `lost`, if the guards pass |
| `maintenance.max` | 24 h | cap on a maintenance window |

A drive is lost after minutes and a node after a day because a `failed` drive is a definite fact (the owner wrote `FAILED`) and its declustered rebuild takes about 2 hours, while a node that is down is most often a crash and reboot, and a node's loss costs 47.6 TB of writes and 71.4 TB of reads at the guard (10.4). **This timer no longer gates repair of exposed data: margin-triggered repair (24.5) starts at once for every block below its threshold, whatever the timer says.** The `lost` fact decides only whether the rest of the node's blocks, those still at a safe margin, are rebuilt too.

**Unavailability budget.** A node is eligible for `lost` when its **cumulative** unavailable time in the trailing 48 h reaches `node_down_after`, or it has been continuously unavailable that long. The transition history it is computed from is **persisted** (the node event history on every storage node's NVMe, merged by peers), so a viewer's restart or a power event does not reset it. Time inside a declared maintenance window does not count.

**Flap damper (a preference with a floor).** Per component a score `s`: +1 per unannounced down transition (a clean shutdown announces itself), halving every hour; **downs of two or more nodes within one minute count once** (a correlated event, such as power, is not flapping). `s >= 2` sets `flapping`: last in read preference, not preferred as a hot replica or shard target, returned-processing held. It clears when `s < 1` and the node has been `up` for its probation (15 min after an unannounced restart, 60 s after an announced one; **probation is skipped after a correlated event**). **Floor:** if excluding `flapping` or probation nodes would leave fewer eligible nodes than `hot.min_acks` (hot) or than the stripe invariant needs (cold), the exclusion is ignored for that decision; the damper never makes writes fail. `flapping` for 1 h raises a ticket and proposes a `flapping` fact; **`flapping` for 6 h pages `NodeDownNeedsDecision`**, because on three nodes there is no automatic exit (R4). A writer does not seal-and-roll on a `replica_stall` of a replica that has been `suspect` more than twice in the last hour; it waits `put_copy_stall` instead, so a flapping replica does not fragment segments every few minutes.

**Guards against self-made storms.**
1. At most one node is auto-declared `lost` at a time; a second needs the operator.
2. `lost` for a node is proposed only if `ok-to-out` holds: the survivors can hold the rebuilt shards **below `rebuild.auto_ceiling` (default 0.85), leaving room for one further drive's rebuild**, the resulting layout satisfies `m >= max_shards_per_domain`, and no stripe is already unreadable. Otherwise nothing is automatic and the page says why.
3. Auto declarations pause while any survivor drive is `failed` and not yet rebuilt.
4. **Burst guard:** if more than 2 drives, or 20% of one node's drives, fail within 10 minutes (an HBA, expander or backplane fault), their `lost` declarations are held, the node is treated as `suspect` as a whole, and the operator decides (R9). Drive-level declarations never become a node loss through the side door.
5. `rebuild.node_down_after = 0` means operator-only.

Alerts while a node is down: at +0 `NodeDown` (info, with the count of stripes at margin <= 1); +10 min ticket; +1 h `NodeDownLong` page; +4 h `NodeDownNeedsDecision` page naming the two decisions (wait for return, or R6).

### 24.4 A returning node or drive
Cold shards are immutable and deterministic (a data shard is the block's bytes; parity is a fixed function of the stripe), so a shard whose blake3 matches its descriptor is valid forever; the only staleness is **stripe membership** (a shard of a stripe freed while the component was away must never come back). Hot segments are fenced by ballot, committed length, running crc and seal record (10.3); large objects are immutable files named by generation.

On start, and when a drive reappears, the owner sends a **holdings report** while `indexing`: it lists every hot segment, large object and cold shard it holds and compares each with the block table and descriptors (majority-committed, so any live peer serves them):
- **Known and consistent:** adopted. Every shard file carries its **last-verified time**, persisted in its footer's companion record; a shard verified by scrub within `cold.scrub_period` before the component left, whose size and footer match the descriptor, **stays verified**; any other returning shard gets a cheap footer and size check at once and joins the normal scrub order, not a front-of-queue full read. So after a reboot the verified margin (the series the alerts read) returns within minutes, not after a 14-day scrub.
- **Known but behind** (a hot copy shorter than its committed seal, tombstone cursors behind): converged by the hot restart rules (10.3).
- **Layout debt it can repay by adoption:** for a stripe rebuilt into a degraded layout while this component was away, whose original shard here verifies, a descriptor CAS points the shard back at this component and the rebuilt copy is deleted after that commit. Nothing is copied back. Only a stripe already at its full layout without this shard treats it as **surplus**, kept as a spare for `surplus_grace` (7 days) and then deleted.
- **Unknown** (stripe id not in the descriptors, or freed): quarantined under `orphan/`, never served or counted, deleted after `surplus_grace`.

A `lost` fact deletes nothing from descriptors; descriptors change only when a rebuild commits, so a node declared lost that returns brings back every stripe not yet rebuilt at no copy cost, and the queue shrinks as shards are adopted.

**Retention [D].** Cold shards are adopted by descriptor **however long the component was away**: the descriptors and freed-stripe records define validity, so there is no age limit for them. Only **hot-tier state and key-tombstone cursors** have one: a component away longer than `tombstone_retention` (30 days) discards its hot segments and tombstone cursors and rejoins them fresh (the daemon refuses to start with them and says so; `store node rejoin-fresh` discards them), while its cold shards are still adopted.

**Reads while partitioned.** A node that cannot reach a majority serves reads of what its index holds: keys are create-only and their bytes never change, so a hit is never stale bytes; a key deleted on the majority side may still be served by the minority node until it heals and applies the tombstone. It acknowledges no write and commits nothing.

### 24.5 The repair scheduler
**Shape.** The queue is soft state: a pure function of descriptors, the block table, the ledger and current component states, recomputed at start and on every state change; nothing about it is persisted, so a restart loses no work. The coordinator of an item is `rank(item id)` among live nodes; it plans, and the nodes holding sources and targets execute. Two coordinators doing the same item (a partition, a takeover) both finish and the descriptor CAS lets one commit; the loser deletes its temporary file. Repair has its own thread pool and the `background` class of 10.7; a degraded read only enqueues its stripe.

**What enters the queue [D].** An item enters **as soon as its margin is at or below its threshold, independent of the `lost` timer**:
- a hot block with fewer than `hot.min_acks` live copies, or a cold stripe at margin <= 1 (counting shards on `down`, `failed`, `missing` or `stalled` components as gone), is queued **immediately** and repaired onto live components, recording `layout_debt` when the target layout is degraded;
- a hot block below `hot.replicas` but at or above `min_acks`, or a cold stripe at margin >= 2 below its full layout, is queued only when its components are `lost` or `out` (or when a ledger `lost` fact exists for them), because a reboot returns those shards at no cost.
So on NOW (two hot replicas) a crash queues every dirty block the node held at once, and on LATER a node down puts its 3-shard stripes (margin 1) in the queue at once, onto the two survivors at 4/4 with `layout_debt`; a return then repays the debt by adoption (24.4).

**Priorities (by remaining margin).**

| Class | Items | Share |
|---|---|---|
| P0 | cold margin <= 0; hot block with one live copy | may take `rebuild.p0_share` (75%) of drive time; preempts scrub and every lower class |
| P1 | cold margin 1; hot block below `min_acks` but above one copy | `rebuild.share` (25%) |
| P2 | cold margin >= 2 below the full layout; hot block below `replicas` (only when `lost`/`out`) | `rebuild.share`, after P1 |
| P3 | placement-invariant violations, `layout_debt` repayment by move, surplus cleanup | `rebuild.share / 2` |
| P4 | drain, evacuation, rebalance | `rebalance.share` (10%) |

Within a class: longest exposure first (`degraded_since` in the block record), then block id. A new P0 item interrupts lower work at the next cell boundary.

**One throttle, shared with scrub.** One budget per drive and one per node link, shared by rebuild, evacuation, rebalance **and scrub**, applied to what demand leaves (demand keeps its preemption and the write share of 10.7). Shares are fractions of the drive's sequential rate measured by T0-S (an implausible measurement keeps the defaults and raises an alert). A decode reading `k` shards charges `k` read streams to its sources and one write stream to its target; at most one background stream per source drive, at most `rebuild.max_items` (4) items per node. Every knob lives in the configuration; a runtime override through the operator API is listed with its expiry in `store repair status`. `store repair pause --class P3,P4 --for 2h` pauses classes; P0 is paused only with `--include-p0`. A target above `cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling` (92%) is refused; an item with no target shows `blocked: capacity` and pages `RepairBlockedCapacity`.

**Per-item commit protocol (crash-safe, never deleting the only copy).**
1. Read `k` source shards in windows; verify every cell against its source's footer crc32c; a bad cell excludes that source and another survivor is tried.
2. Decode the missing shard(s); write `repair/<stripe>.<shard>.<descriptor epoch>.tmp` on the target; `fsync` file and directory.
3. Verify: blake3 of the rebuilt shard equals the per-shard blake3 the stripe descriptor records (**the descriptor gains a blake3 per shard at encode time**, an additive field, T-I2; data shards also match the block's blake3 in the seal record).
4. Rename to the final name, write the footer, `fsync`.
5. Commit the descriptor change by majority CAS on the descriptor epoch, refused if the stripe was freed or already repaired.
6. Only after the commit is the old location removed from the descriptor and anything deleted. The function that deletes a shard or copy takes a `Redundancy` witness that only a check "another verified copy, or the committed replacement, exists" can produce.
At start the daemon removes every `*.tmp` under `repair/`; a crash at steps 1 to 5 leaves only a temporary file, after 5 the item is done.

**Salvage [D].** A `failed` drive that still reads in part is the last source for a stripe at margin <= 0. `store drive salvage D` copies its readable cells read-only, verified by cell crc, into repair items of such stripes, and the scheduler uses a `failed` drive as a last-resort source for P0 items only. `store drive safe-to-destroy D` refuses while any stripe at margin <= 0, or with `cell_lost`, depends on D.

**Latent errors at margin 0.** A margin-0 rebuild reads exactly `k` survivors; a latent sector error in a needed cell loses that cell: the stripe is marked `cell_lost` for that range, an alert fires and `store lost-report` names the keys. A pack the CAS service can republish becomes a miss (4.6); nothing is ever served wrong. The defence is the margin-first scrub (24.7).

**Writes during repair.** Hot: one node down on LATER leaves two live nodes, every PUT needs both (`min_acks = 2`), and a second failure makes small PUTs `503 Retry-After`: hot writes never go below `min_acks`, with no sloppy quorum and no hint queue. Cold: new stripes go to live nodes if the invariant holds (4/4 on two nodes is valid at `4+4`) and carry `layout_debt`; `layout_debt_stripes` counts them, and P3 repays them on return (adoption first, a plain copy only for shards that are not there). **Blocks below `hot.replicas` copies are destaged promptly** while any node is down (they are the least protected data), and no other stripe creation is held back. Placement during an outage never targets a `down`, `suspect`, `flapping`, `lost` or `new` component and never passes the fill limit (subject to the damper's floor, 24.3).

### 24.6 Hot-tier specifics
Each hot block's record holds `copies` (components with a verified full copy), `replicas_wanted` and `degraded_since`; `hot_blocks_by_copies` and `hot_under_replicated_blocks` are computed from it. With one of three nodes down a sealed block stays at two copies until the node returns: **expected, not an alert**, while `copies >= min_acks`. After a return only blocks whose durable home is still hot are caught up (sealed and not yet destaged, and large objects not yet destaged), fewest copies first, by a background whole-file copy verified per record and by blake3, at most 2 per node pair, inside the shared link budget. A stale replica (committed length below the seal, ballot below the promise, diverging running crc) is converged and never read.

**A dead NVMe** is, for hot data, the node's loss (10.3), and it also holds the node's index, block table and descriptors: the node's cold shards are intact but unserved until its index is rebuilt (degraded reads meanwhile, the load of a node down). The node returns as a **new incarnation**, non-voting until it has learned the committed state (24.2); its block table and descriptors are copied from a peer, its index rebuilt by `index-sync` and the trailers of its own shards, its holdings adopted (24.4). A periodic snapshot of the block table and descriptors on one of the node's HDDs (every 5 minutes) shortens this; it is a catalogue only and is never used as acceptor or ledger state (R7).

### 24.7 Cold-tier specifics
**Dead drive.** `failed` -> 10 min -> ledger `lost` -> its stripes queued by margin -> declustered rebuild (10.4): about 2.1 hours at the 25% share at the capacity guard [E, 10.4]. Exposure while rebuilding is margin `m - 1` (3 at `4+4`); `exposure > 4 h` at margin <= 1 pages. There is no hot spare and no identity to keep: a replacement drive is a `new` drive that adds capacity.

**Dead node.** Its 3-shard stripes are repaired at once (margin 1, 24.5); the rest follow its `lost` fact (24 h cumulative, or `store node out`). Rebuilding everything onto two nodes reads 71.4 TB and writes 47.6 TB over 30 drives: at the 25% share (47.5 MB/s per drive) the reads bound it at about 14 hours, about 14 to 20 hours at the default share and about 5 hours at the P0 share [E]; C-11 measures it. Replacement hardware (`store node replace`, R6) joins as the same node id in a new incarnation; debt stripes are repaid to it by moves, and stripes rebuilt as 4/4 are valid and moved back to 3/3/2 only as P4 work.

**Scrub.** Every shard is read once per `cold.scrub_period`: 14 days on CMR drives (LATER), 28 on SMR (NOW); at 3.2 TB per drive that is about 2.6 MB/s, about 1.4% of 190 MB/s [E], inside the shared budget. While any component is `down`, stripes at margin <= 1 are scrubbed first (above ordinary scrub, below P0). No new ordinary scrub starts while a P0 to P2 item exists. Scrub runs only below a load gate (demand under 50% of the drive over 1 minute) and an optional hour window. A shard failing verification is quarantined (`.bad`) and its stripe queued; more than `scrub.error_burst` (20) bad shards on one drive in a period marks the drive `failed`. SMART pending and reallocated sector growth sets the drive `suspect-media`, which starts an **evacuation** (one read per shard) rather than a decode.

**Margin-0 alerts.** `cold_stripe_margin{margin="0"} > 0` and `hot_blocks_by_copies{copies="1"} > 0` older than `rebuild.drive_delay` page, with the stripe count and the P0 ETA while a node is down.

### 24.8 Capacity changes (no data moves by default)
- **Adding a storage node (3 to 4):** a membership edit (24.9); the node is `new`, burns in, becomes `up`. New hot segments may place on it (replica sets of 3 of 4); new stripes use the weighted rule over four nodes (`4+4` as 2/2/2/2). Existing hot blocks and stripes do not move. The new node's placement weight is capped at 1.5 times the median free fraction until its fill reaches the median, and at **1.0 times while a node-loss rebuild runs**. Adding it changes no existing majority (24.2: seal and descriptor majorities are over recorded sets; the ledger voters change only when the operator promotes it).
- **Optional rebalance:** `store rebalance plan` (a dry run listing moves) and `store rebalance apply`: whole-shard copies, never a decode, one move per stripe per step, only for stripes at full margin, from nodes above mean fill + 5% to nodes below, at `rebalance.share`, paused while any P0 to P3 item exists; the old shard is deleted after the move commits.
- **Adding drives to a node:** the same, with the cap per drive; `store rebalance --node N` balances within a node on request.
- **Removing hardware:** `drain` creates P4 evacuation items (copies; a decode only for an unreadable shard); `store node ok-to-stop` and `store drive safe-to-destroy` say yes only when every block and stripe with a copy there has full margin without it.

### 24.9 Changing the membership document
The document is applied **only through a validator**, the same code as the operator verbs, before any node acts on it; a failed validation keeps the last good document and alerts. It checks: a compare-and-set on the previous epoch; at most one node or drive state change per epoch; `in` to `out` directly is refused (through `drain` and `safe-to-destroy`, or `store node out` with its `ok-to-out` result); duplicate node ids and drive UUIDs; a capacity within 2x of the probed device; the ledger voter set changes by at most one node; the resulting layout satisfies `m >= max_shards_per_domain`. **`in` to `drain` or `out` takes effect after `membership.apply_delay` (10 minutes)**, shown by `store status` as pending with the command that cancels it; a revert inside the delay cancels every consequence.

### 24.10 Invariants and the audit
| Id | Invariant | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| I1 | Every acknowledged write is readable from at least `min_acks` hot copies until destaged, then from at least `k` verified cold shards | live-key audit and canary reads; `hot_blocks_by_copies`, `cold_stripe_margin_verified` |
| I2 | A stripe's shards are on distinct drives, no node holds more than `max_shards_per_domain`, and `m >= max_shards_per_domain` | placement audit; `placement_violations` |
| I3 | No stale, surplus or freed copy is served or counted | `stale_served_total` (must stay 0); orphan counters |
| I4 | A committed seal or descriptor never changes | `commit_conflicts_total` |
| I5 | The only copy of anything is never deleted | `last_copy_delete_refused_total`; the `Redundancy` witness (24.5) |
| I6 | No placement targets an ineligible component or uses an ineligible map | `placement_epoch_rejects_total` |
| I7 | Every item below its threshold is queued and progresses | `repair_oldest_exposure_seconds{class}`, `repair_stuck_items` (no progress for 15 min), `repair_commit_quorum_stalled` |
| I8 | Repair and scrub stay within their shared budget while reads meet their targets | `repair_share_actual`, demand wait histograms |
| I9 | The ledger agrees across voters | `ledger_epoch{node}`, `ledger_divergence` |
| I10 | Block tables agree across nodes | `group_digest_mismatch` |

Audit layers: (1) continuous margins and copy counts from descriptors, block table and states, in a `nominal` and a `verified` series; (2) scrub; (3) the block-table digest comparison every 10 minutes; (4) the live-key audit of 10.8; (5) **canary objects**, a fixed set of synthetic keys per hot and cold block class read through every path (healthy, degraded, each component masked) once an hour; (6) in the simulator, the daemon's exported margin and copy functions must equal the oracle's ground truth, so an audit that cannot fail goes red. `store health` prints two lines: **serving** (can every acknowledged key be read now) and **protected** (how much is below full redundancy, by margin).

Pages: unreadable keys > 0; margin-0 or one-copy items older than `drive_delay`; `NodeDownLong`; `NodeDownNeedsDecision` (including `flapping` for 6 h); `RepairBlockedCapacity`; `repair_commit_quorum_stalled` over 10 minutes; `ledger_divergence`; `stale_served_total > 0`. Tickets: `flapping` for 1 h; scrub overrun (a drive not scrubbed within 1.5 periods); `layout_debt_stripes` unchanged for 7 days; `repair_stuck_items`; surplus older than 14 days; an expired maintenance window with the node still away; a `failed` drive uncleared for 24 h; SMART growth; a held burst declaration (24.3 guard 4).

### 24.11 Operator verbs
Every verb is an endpoint of one operator API (JSON and gRPC, both complete) and the CLI is generated from its schema; runtime changes go through the API, configuration through files. Mutating verbs take `--reason` (recorded in the node event history and in the ledger fact); destructive ones print the plan and the safety result and act only with `--apply`; none has a flag that skips a safety check. The one escape is the **break-glass held script** of 24.15 item 4 (time-limited, logged, one named check, reviewed afterwards). A step only the owner runs is a held script generated from the same API call. Each refusal and each blocked repair item carries a reason from the closed set of 24.15 item 2.
- `store status`, `store health`; `store node list|get N` (state, incarnation, the last 20 transitions with reasons, flap score, budget used, the time to `lost`); `store node maintenance start N --for 2h` and `end N`; `store node ok-to-stop N`; `store node ok-to-out N`; `store node cordon N` (no placements); `store node out N` (a prepared membership diff; `--apply` also commits the `lost` fact); `store node replace N` (opens a new incarnation); `store node rejoin-fresh N`.
- `store drive list|get D`, `store drive fail D`, `store drive clear D` (after a verifying scrub), `store drive evacuate D`, `store drive salvage D`, `store drive safe-to-destroy D`, `store drive blink D` (locate the slot), `store drive add` (format plan, held), `store drive replace D` (prints R2).
- `store nvme replace N` (prints R7).
- `store repair status [--class]`, `store repair pause|resume --class ... --for`, `store repair priority STRIPE`, `store repair explain STRIPE|BLOCK` (why it is where it is and what blocks it).
- `store rebalance plan|apply|cancel`; `store audit [--keys FILE]`; `store lost-report`; `store ledger list|get` (read only).
Every state is shown with its reason and the time left ("lost in 6h12m unless it returns; ok-to-out: yes"), and every refusal names what blocks it and the verb that unblocks it.

### 24.12 Runbooks
Each: symptoms, what the system does by itself, what the operator does, how to know it is done, what not to do. Times are LATER defaults at the capacity guard [E]; on today's fill (under 2%) every repair takes minutes.

**R1. A hard drive dies.** *Symptoms:* `DriveFailed` ticket; `store drive get D` shows `failed` and why (EIO count or stalls). *Automatic:* D serves nothing, its stripes read degraded; its margin-1 stripes are already queued (none at `4+4` from one drive alone), at +10 min the ledger commits `lost(D)` and every stripe on it is queued; rebuild runs at the 25% share, about 2.1 h. *Operator:* watch `store repair status`; find the slot (`store drive blink D`); when `store drive safe-to-destroy D` says yes, swap the drive, run the held format script, `store drive add`; the new drive goes `new`, burns in, becomes `up`. *Done:* the protected line is clean and the new drive is `up`. *Do not:* pull a drive that says `safe-to-destroy: no`; clear `failed` to retry a dying disk. *NOW:* SMR drives shared with silo rebuild in about a day at a 15% share; the exposure page is at 24 h.

**R2. Replace a hard drive on purpose.** A healthy but `suspect-media` drive: `store drive evacuate D` (copies), wait for `safe-to-destroy: yes`, swap, format, `store drive add`. A pulled-by-mistake healthy drive goes `failed` on its EIOs: reinsert it, let it scrub (`store drive clear D` is allowed after a clean verifying scrub), and nothing is rebuilt if it is back within `drive_delay`.

**R3. A storage node crashes and reboots.** *Symptoms:* `NodeDown` (info). *Automatic:* reads reroute; hot writes need both remaining nodes; margin-1 stripes and blocks below `min_acks` are queued at once (24.5); on return the node is `indexing` (votes as an acceptor at once, 24.2), reconciles its holdings (24.4), repays layout debt by adoption, catches up the dirty hot blocks it missed, serves clients when its index is built, and is `up` after its probation (15 min unannounced, skipped after a correlated event). *Operator:* nothing, unless it flaps (R4) or `NodeDownLong` fires (R6). Read `store node get N` for the crash reason. *Planned reboot:* `store node ok-to-stop N` must say yes; `store node maintenance start N --for 30m --reason ...`; stop the daemon with SIGTERM (it seals its open segments, flushes and announces `leaving`, so peers fail over at once); reboot; maintenance ends when the node is `up`. *Done:* `up`, protected clean. *Do not:* reboot a second node before the first is `up` and caught up.

**R4. A storage node flaps.** *Symptoms:* `flapping` (ticket at 1 h, page at 6 h). *Automatic:* last in reads, not preferred for placements (never below the floor), its unavailable time counts toward the persisted 48 h budget; on three nodes **there is no automatic exit** short of that budget. *Operator:* read the transition history, fix the cause (kernel, NIC, power supply, temperature); park it with `store node cordon N` or `maintenance start N --for 24h`, or decide `store node out N` (R6). *Done:* stable `up` for its probation and the score below 1. *Do not:* look for a verb that clears `flapping`; there is none.

**R5. Add a storage node.** Edit the membership document (it applies after validation, 24.9); `store node get N` shows `new`, then `up`. No data moves; new writes use it at its weight cap. `store rebalance plan` is optional. To make it a ledger voter, add `ledger = true` to it alone in one epoch, after it is `up` and synced.

**R6. A storage node is gone for good (or the decision to treat it so).** *Symptoms:* `NodeDownNeedsDecision` at 4 h. *Decision aid:* `store node ok-to-out N` prints whether the survivors can absorb the rebuild below the 85% auto ceiling, the layout (`4+4` as 4/4 on two nodes), the volume (71.4 TB read, 47.6 TB written), the time (about 14 to 20 h at the default share, about 5 h at the P0 share), and the statement "after this, one more node loss leaves every stripe at margin 0 and no commit possible". *Exits:* (a) **replace**: new hardware joins as `store node replace N` (same id, new incarnation, non-voting until synced); debt is repaid by moves and hot catch-up fills its NVMe, and no full rebuild is needed; (b) **out**: `store node out N`; the rebuild runs onto two nodes, and a fourth node is added when hardware exists (R5). An automatic `lost` happens at 24 h cumulative only if the guards pass. *Two of three nodes gone:* nothing commits; hot writes are `503`; cold reads work only for stripes with at least `k` shards on the survivor; this is an availability incident for the owner, not a repair; do not start anything with one node. *NOW:* with six nodes `node out` is routine (one survivor holds two shards of each affected `4+2` stripe; the cluster again survives one further node).

**R7. Replace the NVMe (the SATA SSD hot partition on NOW).** *Symptoms:* the hot device errors or vanishes; `NodeHotLost`. *Automatic:* the node's hot copies count as gone; blocks below `min_acks` are queued at once; its HDDs are unserved until its index is rebuilt (degraded reads meanwhile). *Operator:* `store node maintenance start N --for 8h --reason nvme`; replace and format (held script: the same node id and a new incarnation; `store nvme replace N` records the old one as superseded); start the daemon: it copies the block table and descriptors from a peer (or loads the HDD snapshot and pulls the difference), rebuilds its index by `index-sync` and its shards' trailers, adopts its holdings, catches up dirty hot blocks, **and votes only after learning the committed state from a reachable majority** (24.2). *Done:* `indexing` over, `hot_blocks_by_copies{copies<2}` clean, adopted shards checked. *Do not:* restore an old NVMe image: an old incarnation is refused and must rejoin fresh.

**R8. Planned maintenance of several nodes.** One node at a time, each with `ok-to-stop` and R3's planned form. A rack or power job that touches two nodes at once is a service window (R10), never a rolling step.

**R9. A drive burst or a reordered bus.** *Several drives of one node fail within minutes:* the burst guard holds their `lost` declarations and pages; check the HBA, expander, backplane and cabling; if the controller is fixed the drives come back as `missing` then adopted; otherwise treat the node as R6. *Drives renamed after a reboot:* nothing to do (mounted by UUID). *Two drives with one UUID (a clone):* both are `failed` with reason `duplicate-uuid`, never merged.

**R10. Two storage nodes go down together (a power event) [D].** *Symptoms:* two `NodeDown` within a minute; one live node. *Automatic:* nothing commits (no majority); hot PUTs answer `503 Retry-After`; hot reads of segments with a copy on the survivor are served; cold reads fail (the survivor holds at most 3 of 8 shards of a stripe) and the fetch engine waits (non-definitive misses, 4.6), so jobs are delayed, not failed; no repair starts. *On power return:* each node starts, is `indexing` and votes as an acceptor at once from its persisted state, so seals and the ledger commit as soon as two nodes are back; hot restart converges segments (adopt committed seals, cut uncommitted tails); clients are served as each index is built; the downs count as one correlated event (no flap score, no probation). *Operator:* restore power; start the nodes in any order; check `store health` until both lines are clean. Nothing else is needed: no acknowledged write is lost (every acknowledged record was durable on two NVMe devices). *Do not:* mark anything `out` or start a rebuild while nodes are coming back; do not run a planned reboot on a third node in the same window.

### 24.13 Tests: simulator, fault injection, drills
**Deterministic cluster simulator (T-S14, the primary test).** One process, single-threaded and seeded: N storage nodes whose production state machines (ledger, repair scheduler, reconciliation, hot replication, placement, membership validation) run against simulated disk, network and clock traits, so any seed reproduces its run (in the manner of FoundationDB's simulation testing). Faults: a crash at any step boundary (every step of 24.5's commit protocol included), drive EIO, latent sector errors, stalls, symmetric and asymmetric partitions, message delay, duplication and reordering, clock skew, full disks, power loss of all nodes or of two, a long-absent drive, an NVMe replaced or restored from an old image, flapping at chosen periods, a drive burst, and a double fault during rebuild. Workload: PUT, GET and DELETE with destage, GC and promote, against an oracle of every acknowledged write. **Checked after every event:** I1 (every acknowledged key reads correctly within tolerance, and beyond it fails only as `503` or in `store lost-report`, never with wrong bytes), I2 to I6, I9, I10; **liveness** (with faults stopped, every queue empties and margins return to full within a bound set by data and shares in simulated steps); **boundedness** (repair concurrency and shares never exceed their budgets). The same seed gives the same trace; a fixed seed list runs on every PR and a large random batch runs on the farm, not per PR.

Named scenarios, each stating what it proves: (a) a reboot inside the budget copies nothing beyond margin-triggered items, and those are repaid by adoption; (b) a node flapping every 2 minutes for 30 h reaches the persisted budget, and a viewer restart in the middle does not reset it; (c) a minority cannot commit a fact or acknowledge a hot write; (d) a node declared lost returns mid-rebuild and the queue shrinks by adoption with no copy-back; (e) a returned shard of a freed stripe is never served; (f) a crash at each commit step leaves only temporary files and never deletes the only copy; (g) a margin-0 rebuild meeting a latent error reports `cell_lost` and serves no wrong byte; (h) a 4/4 layout is accepted only when `m >= max_shards_per_domain` and is repaid by adoption or moves with no decode; (i) a component away past `tombstone_retention` rejoins its hot state fresh while its cold shards are adopted; (j) hot writes below `min_acks` answer `503`; (k) an old-incarnation NVMe image is refused, and a new incarnation does not vote on any earlier instance until it has learned it, and stalls rather than voting when no majority is reachable; (l) the ledger converges after arbitrary message loss; (m) **a margin-1 stripe is queued at once when a node goes down, before any `lost` fact**; (n) **two nodes rebooting within a minute (R10)**: no commit while one node is up, commits resume with two, no flap score, no probation, no acknowledged write lost; (o) **all nodes flapping together never leave fewer eligible targets than `min_acks`** (the damper's floor); (p) **adding a fourth node during a node-loss rebuild** changes no seal, descriptor or ledger majority and does not stall the rebuild; an unrelated fact does not restart an in-flight item; (q) **a drive burst** on one node holds its declarations; (r) **the membership validator** refuses `in` to `out`, two state changes in one epoch, duplicate ids and UUIDs, a capacity off by more than 2x and a voter-set change of two nodes, and a revert inside `apply_delay` cancels every consequence; (s) **salvage**: a partly readable `failed` drive restores a margin-0 stripe, and `safe-to-destroy` refuses while it is needed; (t) **verified margin after a reboot** returns to its pre-outage value without a full re-scrub; (u) **a node on a fresh hot device allocates no block id it ever used before**; (v) **misdirected writes and byte corruption** (on disk and on the wire) are detected and repaired and never served; (w) **mass return and mass restart** (every node and drive back at once, and 50 restarts in a row) trigger no deletion and leave every queue drained in the quiet phase; (x) **the planted-corruption fixture** (a shard corrupted behind the code's back) turns the run red if nothing catches it; (y) **the capacity invariant** stops admission at the post-loss ceiling and a node loss afterwards rebuilds fully; (z) **a slow drive** (a fraction of its speed, no errors) is avoided for reads and evacuated, and a clock skew beyond `clock.max_skew` stops ledger proposals from that node.

**Real-code fault injection.** The fake storage node of T-I with scripted hangs, crashes, EIO and slow answers, on real sockets on loopback and real files in the test's scratch directory, with injected clocks; it covers the drive scheduler, the peer protocol and the real file formats.

**Farm drills (T-S15; real hardware, held steps, never per PR).** Each drill is a Rust binary run through hostkeeper or as a held step, first on the LATER cluster before production traffic; on NOW only the reboot and single-drive drills. Pass criteria are metric states, never durations. 1. `kill -9` the daemon during a rebuild: the queue is recomputed, temporary files removed, margins return to full, I1 holds. 2. Power-cycle one node under load: no full rebuild, debt repaid by adoption, verified margin restored, no unreadable keys. 3. **Power-cycle two nodes together (R10)**: as scenario (n). 4. Offline a drive through the kernel interface during reads: `failed`, `lost` after the timer, the rebuild completes at its share, demand latency within 4.3's targets. 5. Partition one node: the minority acknowledges nothing, the majority proceeds, the ledger reconverges. 6. A flap generator for an hour: `flapping` per the rule, nothing but margin-triggered repair copied. 7. Add a drive and a node: no existing shard moves, the ramp cap holds, a rebalance plan is correct and abortable. 8. NVMe replacement on a spare node: R7 end to end. 9. A full node-lost rebuild onto two nodes before production, measured (it replaces the estimates of 24.7). 10. A soak with the simulator's fault schedule, the canaries and the live-key audit: zero invariant violations. 11. **Cold start, once before first production:** power all three storage nodes off and on, and restore one node's catalogue from the off-box copy (24.15 item 7). 12. **Asymmetric partition:** traffic broken in one direction between one pair of nodes. Drills after production run quarterly, owned by whoever owns releases, with an alert when the last run is older than 120 days. Results are metrics and a report, and their numbers replace [E] values here.

### 24.14 What the review required, and where it is resolved
| Review finding | Resolution | Where |
|---|---|---|
| X1: the P0 class and the `lost` timer contradicted each other | Margin-triggered repair: below-threshold items are queued at once, independent of `lost` | 24.5, scenario (m) |
| X4 / F-AMNESIA: a blank or restored acceptor could vote again | New incarnations and restored state are non-voting until they learn from a reachable majority; otherwise a safe stall; snapshots are catalogue only | 24.2, 24.6, R7, scenario (k) |
| F-VOTERS: voter sets undefined; adding a node could stall commits | Seal majority over the recorded replica set, descriptor majority over the recorded placement, ledger voters an explicit list changed one at a time after sync | 24.2, 24.8, R5, scenario (p) |
| F-EPOCH / X5: every fact restarted in-flight placements | Refusal only when the diff changes the item's eligibility | 24.1, scenario (p) |
| X2 / F-FLAP1-3: the damper could remove every target; no exit; budget reset by restarts | Preference with a floor; correlated downs count once; page at 6 h; persisted history | 24.3, R4, scenarios (b), (o) |
| F-BOOT1-2 and R10: two nodes rebooting together | Acceptor duty in `indexing`; probation skipped after a correlated event; runbook R10 | 24.2, 24.3, R10, scenario (n), drill 3 |
| F-VALIDATE / F-DELAY: hand-edited membership could do harm | Validator with CAS, one change per epoch, no direct `out`, duplicate and capacity checks; a 10-minute apply delay with cancel | 24.9, scenario (r) |
| F-SALVAGE: a failed drive was the last source and was forbidden | `store drive salvage`; last-resort source for P0; `safe-to-destroy` refuses while needed | 24.5, R1, scenario (s) |
| F-BURST: an HBA fault became a node loss through drive declarations | Burst guard holds declarations and pages | 24.3, R9, scenario (q) |
| F-ADOPT / F-VERIFY / F-TOMB: about 47 TB of avoidable copies; verified-margin noise; retention wiping cold shards | Debt repaid by adopting the returning copy; persisted last-verified time; retention for hot state and tombstone cursors only | 24.4, scenarios (d), (i), (t) |
| X3: three-node commits need both survivors | Stated plainly, `repair_commit_quorum_stalled` metric and page; a fourth node is the fix | 24 (opening), 24.10 |
| F-CEIL: auto-lost could leave no room for the next drive | `rebuild.auto_ceiling` 0.85 for automatic `lost` | 24.3 guard 2 |
| F-MINORITY-READ | Minority nodes serve create-only keys (never stale bytes; a deleted key until heal) and acknowledge nothing | 24.4 |
| Seal-and-roll churn under flapping | No seal-and-roll for a replica `suspect` more than twice in an hour | 24.3 |
| Destage urgency with a node down | Blocks below `hot.replicas` are destaged promptly; nothing else is held back | 24.5 |

**What this section changes elsewhere (all in T-I2 or the tasks of section 18):** the stripe descriptor gains a blake3 per shard (an additive field of its next version); the `NODE` identity file gains the incarnation; the header `x-ts-ledger-epoch`; the ledger fact record and its single-decree messages on the existing seal route's agreement code; the configuration keys `rebuild.p0_share`, `rebuild.auto_ceiling`, `rebuild.drive_missing_after`, `rebuild.node_down_after` (now cumulative), `rebuild.max_items`, `maintenance.max`, `tombstone_retention`, `surplus_grace`, `scrub.*`, `rebalance.share`, `membership.apply_delay`, `ledger.takeover`, `new_node_weight_cap` and `ledger = true` in the membership document; and 10.13's risks gain "the NVMe holds the metadata of 15 HDDs (24.6)" and "after a node loss `4+4` has no node-loss margin until a fourth node exists".

### 24.15 What to build first for operations, and the lessons it rests on [D]
A study of public storage incidents and practice (the S3 outage of 2017, Cloudflare R2's incidents of February and March 2025, DigitalOcean's NYC3 Spaces incident, GitLab's 2017 database outage, Dropbox's Magic Pocket papers on verification, deletion checking and failover testing, Backblaze's drive statistics, TigerBeetle's simulation testing, Huang et al. on gray failure, and Ceph's operations documentation), followed by a review sized to three storage nodes and a small team, gives ten additions in priority order (data loss or silent stall prevented per unit of work). **Scale rule:** each is a small mechanism inside the existing daemon, its verbs and its simulator; nothing here is a second control plane (no plan-hashing framework, no separate referee daemon, no runbook workflow engine, no durability-budget freeze). Each names the failure it prevents and the test that shows it.

1. **The four unknowns, resolved in this document.**
   - *Block ids are never reused.* A `block_id` is (node id, per-node counter) (10.2); the counter is persisted with the replay mark and never decreases, and a node starting on a fresh hot device (a new incarnation, 24.2) first learns the highest id its node id ever allocated from its peers' block tables and allocates above it, so an id names one block forever. Test: simulator scenario (u).
   - *A key's bytes never change while it is live.* Keys are create-only: a PUT of a different generation under a live key is `412` (10.2, 10.9), so no write ever overwrites live bytes; a re-PUT after a DELETE is a new life with a higher stamp (10.2), and the CAS service's pack keys are content-derived. The deletion guard of item 4 therefore covers every way a live object's bytes can go.
   - *Space is reclaimed* by GC: stripe frees and rewrites (10.5), and the `cold_fill_efficiency` metric shows what is dead.
   - *Index coverage:* on LATER (`hot.replicas = 3` on three nodes) every storage node indexes every key; on NOW (`hot.replicas = 2` on six nodes) each key is indexed on its two replica nodes, so `LOCATE` walks `rank(K)` (10.6).
   - *The durability target and failure model* are the owner's decision (Q5 in the decision table); this document is written against its default.
2. **Margin-first repair (24.5) and a closed set of blocking reasons.** Every item that is not progressing carries exactly one reason from a closed set, each with its one-line next action, shown by `store repair status` and `store repair explain`: `capacity` (add capacity or free space, 24.8), `no-quorum` (a commit waits for a second node: bring it back, 24), `no-source` (fewer than `k` readable shards: `store drive salvage`, 24.5), `target-ineligible` (every target is `flapping`, `new` or full), `paused` (who paused it and until when), `budget` (waiting for its share: nothing to do), `burst-hold` (operator decision, R9), `maintenance` (the window's end). A new reason is a code change and a new line in this list. Test: every reason is produced by a simulator scenario, and `explain` names it.
3. **The capacity invariant: fill after losing the largest failure domain stays under the ceiling.** The projected fill of the survivors if the largest failure domain (on LATER a node; per drive within a node) were lost and rebuilt must stay below `cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling`; this replaces the flat 92% as the capacity rule. **PUT admission enforces it from day one**: when a new stripe could no longer be placed without breaking it, destage holds and the hot marks of 10.5 take over (503, then 507), so the store refuses writes before it loses the room to repair. `store_days_to_full` (the guard's free logical bytes over the trailing 7-day net write rate) tickets below `capacity.lead_time` (default 60 days, the hardware lead time) and pages below 14 days. Test: a simulator scenario fills the cluster to the invariant and asserts admission stops there, and a node loss afterwards rebuilds fully.
4. **One narrow guard on the four destructive primitives, trash before delete, node-scoped limits.** One `guard` function, with a witness type only it can produce, sits in front of exactly four primitives: deleting a shard or copy, marking a node or drive `lost`, applying a membership document, and purging trash. It re-checks current state at apply (no plan hashing): a delete is scoped by `(block, generation, drive)` and needs the `Redundancy` witness (24.5); `lost` passes the guards of 24.3; a membership document passes 24.9; a purge is allowed only for records older than the retention. **Deletes go to `trash/` first:** a deleted shard, copy or large file is renamed into `trash/` on its own drive with a record (what, why, which verb or timer), kept 7 days (`trash.retention`), then purged; scrub's audit pass alerts on any unlink that has no trash record. **Node-scoped limits:** no action takes a stripe below margin 1; one node out at a time; the drive-burst hold (24.3). `ok-to-stop` is enforced by the daemon (on SIGTERM it seals and flushes, and if `ok-to-stop` is false it logs the refusal, keeps serving for `stop.grace` and stops only when it becomes true or the grace ends) and by the update hook (which does not stop a node that is not `ok-to-stop`). **Break-glass:** a held script, time-limited and logged, that overrides one named guard check once; reviewed afterwards. Tests: a planted wrong delete is refused by the guard and, with the guard bypassed in a fixture, caught by the trash audit; a 50-node mass restart in the simulator (scenario (w)) triggers no deletion.
5. **Simulator additions that find real bugs (T-S14):** a two-phase run (a fault phase, then a quiet phase in which every queue must drain and every margin return: the liveness check of 24.13); **misdirected writes** (a write that lands at the wrong offset or file) and **byte corruption** on disk and on the wire; **mass return** (every node and drive back at once after a long outage); and **a planted-corruption fixture that must go red**: a seed that corrupts a shard behind the code's back, run on every PR, fails the run if no invariant or audit catches it.
6. **Verified reads on every path, and an independent canary prober.** Every byte that moves is verified where it lands: client fills by cell digest (3.3), `digest=none` objects by the serving node's cell crc (9), repair sources and outputs (24.5), evacuation and rebalance copies, hot whole-file copies (`SEG-COPY`), salvage and adoption (24.4) by per-cell crc and per-shard blake3. **The prober** is a separate small process (`ts-prober`, its own crate) that uses only the public paths (the S3 front and a FUSE mount): it keeps a fixed corpus of known-content objects per hot and cold class and reads each hourly through every path (healthy, degraded, with a component masked by a debug header, through FUSE), and reads a few random old objects verified by hash; its results go to the alerting of hostkeeper, not through tierstore's metrics, and a **dead-man alert** pages when no prober success has arrived for 2 hours. It survives tierstore's failure, not the loss of the box, switch or power it runs on (it runs on a build node).
7. **An off-box copy of the ledger and descriptors, and one cold-start drill before production.** Every hour each storage node exports the ledger, the block table and the stripe descriptors (small: about a few hundred thousand records, 10.2) to `backup.target`, a location off the three storage nodes (a directory on a build node, written through hostkeeper, or an object store outside the cluster), with a checksum and the epochs. This is the answer to losing all three NVMe devices at once: descriptors are also rebuildable from shard footers (10.2), and the copy makes that a restore. A ledger restored from it is non-voting (24.2), so returning the cluster to service from it is a held step: with every storage node stopped, `store ledger reseed --from-backup` starts a new ledger era from the copy at a higher ballot, and the nodes then reconcile their holdings (24.4). **Drill:** once on the farm before first production, power all three storage nodes off, bring them back, and once restore a node's catalogue from the off-box copy (drill 11 of 24.13).
8. **An operator path that does not need tierstore, and jitter.** `tierstore store status --local` reads the node's own state files (the ledger copy, the last exported metrics, the drive and node states, the event history) directly from disk, with no daemon and no network, so an operator can see a node whose daemon will not start. **Jitter:** `ledger.takeover`, the backoff of a repair item with no target, client retries (4.6) and daemon restarts after a crash all add a random 0 to 50% to their delay, so many nodes or clients never retry in step.
9. **Hardware audit.** At start and daily each storage node records, per device: model, firmware, the volatile write-cache setting, whether the device reports power-loss protection, and the flush behaviour; it exports them and alerts on a write cache enabled on a device without power-loss protection whose flushes are not honoured, and on two firmware versions of one model in the cluster. It refuses nothing. (DigitalOcean's NYC3 incident was a drive cache setting.)
10. **Upgrades as a rule and a checklist, and a format policy.** Every storage-node upgrade is a controlled single-node failure, scheduled as one: one node at a time; `ok-to-stop` first; after the node is back, a completed scrub of its margin-1 stripes before the next node; the previous binary kept for rollback. **Format policy:** no on-start migration of on-disk formats (a new format is written by new writes and by background rewrite only), and every release reads the formats of the next one where they changed additively (N reads N+1 for the wire, ledger and descriptor formats), with mixed-version tests for those formats only. Upgrading one node of three is honest about its cost: while it is away, 3-shard stripes run at margin 1 and hot writes need both other nodes.

**Lessons added to the mechanisms above.**
- **Slow disk, not only dead disk.** The commonest HDD failure is a drive that still answers at a fraction of its speed. Each storage node compares each drive's read service time with the median of the drives of the same model on the node; a drive above 3 times that median for 10 minutes is `slow`: the scheduler stops sending it reads that another copy or a degraded read can serve more cheaply, the client's budget treats it as in decrease (4.3), and after 24 hours as `slow` an evacuation starts (24.7). Peer-observed latency (from `x-ts-load` and part responses) is compared with the drive's own report, so a node that answers but is sick (gray failure) is visible as a gap between the two.
- **Clocks.** Every timer, lease, `degraded_since` and damper window uses the monotonic clock; wall time is used only inside the hybrid logical clock (10.2) and in logs. A node whose wall clock differs from its peers' by more than `clock.max_skew` (default 1 s) proposes no ledger fact and alerts; a monotonic jump larger than the ping interval (a resume from suspend, a stalled VM) makes the node re-validate its view before it acts on any timer.
- **Asymmetric partitions.** A drill (drill 12 of 24.13) breaks traffic in one direction between one pair of nodes, the gray failure a bad optic produces; the acceptor view checks of 24.2 and the client-local `suspect` of 10.8 are what must hold.
- **Repair and scrub never stall a serving drive.** Background work yields on the drive's queue, not only by a byte budget: no background stream starts on a drive whose demand queue is non-empty or whose demand wait is above its baseline, at most one background stream per drive at a time (24.5), and a running one yields at the next chunk boundary (10.7).

**Deferred until there is evidence or a fourth node:** a durability budget with automatic freezes, a runbook workflow engine, placement spread across drive models, a separate deletion-referee daemon, long-horizon re-reads of sampled writes, and reference models beyond the hot segment append and seal and the shard write-fsync-rename-footer protocol.
